\title{Erd\H{o}s's conjecture on large totient fibers, conditional on smooth shifted primes: a formal verification}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
For a positive integer $n$ let $g(n)$ be the number of $m\ge1$ with $\varphi(m)=n$. Erd\H{o}s conjectured that for every $\varepsilon>0$ the inequality $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$ holds for infinitely many $n$. The source paper derives this from a lower bound for primes $p$ in $(2x,5x]$ with $p-1$ being $x^\delta$-smooth, by the product-and-pigeonhole argument of Pomerance. We record a Lean~4/Mathlib verification of that derivation, with the smooth-shifted-primes bound taken as an explicit hypothesis. The verification also proves, unconditionally, that every totient fiber is finite. The quantitative upper bound $g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}$ from the source's Lemma~1 is not formalized, and the proof does not need it.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Statement}

Let $\varphi$ be Euler's totient function. For a positive integer $n$ put
\[
 g(n)=\#\{m\ge1:\ \varphi(m)=n\}.
\]
For an integer $m>1$, $P^+(m)$ denotes its largest prime factor. We say $m$ is \emph{$y$-smooth} if $P^+(m)\le y$.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Hypothesis (H)} (smooth shifted primes; Theorem~1.2 of the source paper, taken here as an assumption).
For every fixed $0<\delta<1/4$ and every $\eta>0$ there is $x_0$ such that for all real $x\ge x_0$,
\[
 \#\{p:\ p\text{ prime},\ 2x<p\le 5x,\ P^+(p-1)\le x^{\delta}\}\ \ge\ x^{1-\eta}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Erd\H{o}s's conjecture on large totient fibers, conditional on (H)]
Assume (H). Then for every real $\varepsilon>0$ there are infinitely many positive integers $n$ with $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$.
\end{theorem}

Along the way the source proves the following unconditional statement (Lemma~1): each fiber $\{m:\varphi(m)=n\}$ is finite, and $g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}$ for every $\eta>0$.

\section{Formalization}

The formalization uses Mathlib. From the library it takes the following objects:
\begin{itemize}
\item the totient $\varphi$;
\item the set $\pf(m)$ of prime factors of $m\in\N$, with $\pf(0)=\pf(1)=\emptyset$;
\item the $p$-adic valuation $v_p(m)$;
\item the cardinality $\operatorname{Nat.card}$ of a type, which is defined to be $0$ for infinite types;
\item the real logarithm and the real power $x^y$;
\item the natural floor $\nfl{y}$, equal to $\lfloor y\rfloor$ for $y\ge0$ and to $0$ for $y<0$;
\item the binary logarithm $\lgt(N)$, the largest $L$ with $2^L\le N$ when $N\ge1$, and $\lgt(0)=0$.
\end{itemize}
Natural numbers are cast to $\R$ silently in what follows. In $\N$ the expression $p-1$ is truncated subtraction. For a prime $p$ it is the ordinary $p-1$.

\begin{definition}[fiber size]
For $n\in\N$ let $g(n)=\operatorname{Nat.card}\{m\in\N:\ 0<m,\ \varphi(m)=n\}$.
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{totientFiberCard}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ `g(n) = \PYZsh{}\PYZob{}m ≥ 1 : φ(m) = n\PYZcb{}`. The fiber is finite (Lemma 1). If it were infinite,}
\PY{l+s+sd}{`Nat.card` would return `0`, which could only make the theorem harder. }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{noncomputable}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{totientFiberCard}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n}{Nat}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{card}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{/}\PY{n+nb+bp}{/}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{\PYZlt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∧}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Nat}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{totient}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{=}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{\PYZcb{}}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}

If a fiber were infinite, this definition would give $g(n)=0$. \Cref{lem:totient_fiber_finite} shows that every fiber is finite, so $g(n)$ is the true count. For $n=0$ the fiber is empty.

\begin{definition}[smoothness]
For $m\in\N$ and $y\in\R$, $m$ is \emph{$y$-smooth} if every $q\in\pf(m)$ satisfies $q\le y$.
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{IsSmoothNat}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ `m` is `y`}\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{smooth: every prime factor of `m` is at most `y`.}
\PY{l+s+sd}{(For `m \PYZgt{} 1` this is `P⁺(m) ≤ y`.) }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{IsSmoothNat}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{m}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℕ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{y}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℝ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kt}{Prop}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}
\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∀}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{q}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{∈}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{m}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{primeFactors}\PY{o}{,}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{q}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℝ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{y}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}

For $m>1$ this says exactly $P^+(m)\le y$. The integers $0$ and $1$ are $y$-smooth for every $y$. This edge case is irrelevant: (H) only matters for large $x$, since $x_0$ may be enlarged, and the proof uses it only with $x\ge1$. There $p>2x\ge2$, so $p-1>1$.

\begin{definition}[Hypothesis (H)]
\emph{(H)} is the proposition: for all reals $\delta,\eta$ with $0<\delta<1/4$ and $\eta>0$ there exists $x_0\in\R$ such that for all real $x\ge x_0$,
\[
 \#\{p\in\N:\ p\text{ prime},\ 2x<p\le 5x,\ p-1\text{ is }x^{\delta}\text{-smooth}\}\ \ge\ x^{1-\eta}.
\]
\end{definition}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{SmoothShiftedPrimesHypothesis}}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\},breaklines=true]
\PY{l+s+sd}{/\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{ Hypothesis (H): for every `0 \PYZlt{} δ \PYZlt{} 1}\PY{l+s+sd}{/}\PY{l+s+sd}{4` and every `η \PYZgt{} 0` there is `x₀` such that}
\PY{l+s+sd}{for all real `x ≥ x₀`, `\PYZsh{}\PYZob{}p prime : 2x \PYZlt{} p ≤ 5x, P⁺(p}\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{1) ≤ x\PYZca{}δ\PYZcb{} ≥ x\PYZca{}(1}\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+sd}{η)`. }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZhy{}/}
\PY{k+kn}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{SmoothShiftedPrimesHypothesis}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kt}{Prop}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}

The counted set lies in $[0,5x]$, so it is finite and $\operatorname{Nat.card}$ is its true size. This is Hypothesis (H) word for word.

It takes (H) as a hypothesis, which stands for Theorem~1.2 of the source. It also takes a real $\varepsilon>0$. It asserts that the set
\[
 \{n\in\N:\ 0<n,\ g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}\}
\]
is infinite. Since $g$ is the true fiber size, this is exactly the source theorem. The theorem \leanname{solution} below has precisely this form.

Of the source's Lemma~1, only the finiteness of fibers is formalized (\cref{lem:totient_fiber_finite}). The bound $g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}$ is not.

\section{Proof}

\subsection{Overview}

The formal proof follows the product-and-pigeonhole argument of the source, with these steps:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \Cref{lem:totient_fiber_finite} proves that fibers are finite. \Cref{lem:card_le_totientFiberCard} turns this into a usable lower bound for $g(n)$.
\item \Cref{lem:totient_prod_primes,lem:prod_primes_inj} give the identity $\varphi(\prod_{p\in Q}p)=\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$ and the injectivity of $Q\mapsto\prod_{p\in Q}p$ (unique factorization).
\item \Cref{lem:exists_large_fiber} is the pigeonhole step. If all $k$-subsets of a set $S$ of primes have their values $\prod(p-1)$ in a finite set $V$, then some value $n$ satisfies $\binom{|S|}{k}\le|V|\,g(n)$.
\item \Cref{lem:smooth_count,lem:prod_smooth_bounded} make estimate~(2) of the source precise. A product of $k$ shifted primes $p-1\le N$ that are $y$-smooth is a $y$-smooth integer in $(0,N^k]$. There are at most $(\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{y}}$ such integers.
\item \Cref{lem:choose_ge_pow_div} is the binomial inequality~(1).
\item \Cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth_logbound} through \Cref{lem:asymp_main} carry out the asymptotic comparison of (1) and (2) with explicit constants. This replaces the source's ``$X^{o(k)}$''.
\item \Cref{lem:exists_prime_finset,lem:good_n_from_finset,lem:exists_good_n_large,lem:infinite_of_lt_one} assemble the argument.
\end{enumerate}

The formal proof deviates from the source in the following points.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Scale.} The source passes to $X=5x$ and to primes $p\le X$. The formal proof works directly with the parameter $x$ of (H), the primes in $(2x,5x]$, and $N=\nfl{5x}$.
\item \emph{Parameters.} It fixes $\delta=\varepsilon/8$, applies (H) with $\eta=\delta/2$, and takes $k=\nfl{x^{2\delta}}$.
\item \emph{Counting smooth values.} The number of possible values is bounded by $(\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}$. The number of primes $\le y$ is bounded by $\nfl{y}$, and every exponent by $\lgt(N^k)$.
\item \emph{Infinitude.} The source deduces unboundedness of the produced $n$ from Lemma~1. The formal proof instead shows directly that the produced $n$ exceeds $x$: $n$ is divisible by some $p-1$ with $p>2x$. So the quantitative part of Lemma~1 is never needed, and is not formalized.
\item \emph{Large $\varepsilon$.} The case $\varepsilon\ge1$ is reduced to $\varepsilon=1/2$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{totient_fiber_finite}]\label{lem:totient_fiber_finite}
For every $n\in\N$ the set $\{m\in\N:\ 0<m,\ \varphi(m)=n\}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n}{exact}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{hB\PYZus{}set}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{subset}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{h\PYZus{}subset}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Case $n=0$.} The set is empty, since $\varphi(m)=0$ only for $m=0$.

\emph{Case $n>0$.} Let $m>0$ with $\varphi(m)=n$. We show $m\le B:=(n+1)^{(n+2)n}$.

Write $m=\prod_{p\in\pf(m)}p^{a_p}$ with $a_p=v_p(m)\ge1$. Since $p^{a_p}\mid m$, we have $\varphi(p^{a_p})\mid\varphi(m)=n$. Also $\varphi(p^{a_p})=p^{a_p-1}(p-1)$. This gives two consequences:
\begin{itemize}
\item $p-1\mid n$, so $p\le n+1$;
\item $p^{a_p-1}\mid n$, so $a_p\le 2^{a_p-1}\le p^{a_p-1}\le n$. Here $a\le 2^{a-1}$ for $a\ge1$ is proved by induction.
\end{itemize}
Every prime factor of $m$ lies in $\{0,\dots,n+1\}$, so $|\pf(m)|\le n+2$. Hence
\[
 m=\prod_{p}p^{a_p}\le\prod_p(n+1)^{a_p}=(n+1)^{\sum_p a_p}\le(n+1)^{(n+2)n}=B.
\]
The fiber is therefore contained in the finite set $\{0,\dots,B\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{card_le_totientFiberCard}]\label{lem:card_le_totientFiberCard}
Let $n\in\N$ and let $T\subset\N$ be finite. Suppose every $m\in T$ satisfies $0<m$ and $\varphi(m)=n$. Then $|T|\le g(n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:totient_fiber_finite} the fiber is finite, so $g(n)$ is its cardinality. The inclusion of $T$ into the fiber is injective, which gives $|T|\le g(n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{totient_prod_primes}]\label{lem:totient_prod_primes}
Let $Q\subset\N$ be a finite set of primes. Then $\varphi\bigl(\prod_{p\in Q}p\bigr)=\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We use induction on $Q$. The empty case is $\varphi(1)=1$.

For $Q\cup\{p\}$ with $p\notin Q$: the product $\prod_{q\in Q}q$ is coprime to $p$, because each $q\in Q$ is a prime different from $p$. By multiplicativity of $\varphi$, the induction hypothesis, and $\varphi(p)=p-1$,
\[
 \varphi\Bigl(p\prod_{q\in Q}q\Bigr)=(p-1)\prod_{q\in Q}(q-1).\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{prod_primes_inj}]\label{lem:prod_primes_inj}
Let $Q_1,Q_2\subset\N$ be finite sets of primes with $\prod_{p\in Q_1}p=\prod_{p\in Q_2}p$. Then $Q_1=Q_2$.
\end{lemma}

\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
For a finite set $Q$ of primes, $\pf\bigl(\prod_{p\in Q}p\bigr)=Q$ (Mathlib's \texttt{Nat.primeFactors\_prod}). Hence
\[
 Q_1=\pf\Bigl(\prod_{Q_1}p\Bigr)=\pf\Bigl(\prod_{Q_2}p\Bigr)=Q_2.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{exists_large_fiber}]\label{lem:exists_large_fiber}
Let $S\subset\N$ be a finite set of primes, let $k\in\N$ with $k\le|S|$, and let $V\subset\N$ be finite. Suppose that for every $Q\subseteq S$ with $|Q|=k$ we have $\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)\in V$. Then there is $Q\subseteq S$ with $|Q|=k$ and
\[
 \binom{|S|}{k}\le |V|\cdot g\Bigl(\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)\Bigr).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $T$ be the family of $k$-subsets of $S$, so $|T|=\binom{|S|}{k}\ge1$ since $k\le|S|$. Let $f(Q)=\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$. By hypothesis $f$ maps $T$ into $V$. Hence
\[
 |T|=\sum_{v\in V}|T_v|,\qquad T_v=\{Q\in T: f(Q)=v\}.
\]

The set $V$ is nonempty: otherwise $T$ would be empty, contradicting $|T|\ge1$. Choose $n\in V$ with $|T_n|$ maximal. Then $|T|\le|V|\,|T_n|$. In particular $T_n\neq\emptyset$, since $|T|>0$. Pick $Q\in T_n$, so $f(Q)=n$.

It remains to show $|T_n|\le g(n)$. The map $Q'\mapsto\prod_{p\in Q'}p$ is injective on $T_n$ by \cref{lem:prod_primes_inj}. Each image is positive, and by \cref{lem:totient_prod_primes} its totient is $f(Q')=n$. By \cref{lem:card_le_totientFiberCard} the image has at most $g(n)$ elements, so $|T_n|\le g(n)$. Therefore
\[
 \binom{|S|}{k}\le|V|\,g(n).\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{smooth_count}]\label{lem:smooth_count}
Let $N\in\N$ and $y\in\R$. There is a finite set $V\subset\N$ with
\[
 |V|\le(\lgt(N)+1)^{\nfl{y}}
\]
such that every $n\in\N$ with $0<n\le N$ that is $y$-smooth lies in $V$.
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
Let $P$ be the set of primes in $[1,\nfl{y}]$, so $|P|\le\nfl{y}$. Let $V$ be the image of the map
\[
 e\ \longmapsto\ \prod_{p\in P}p^{e(p)},\qquad e:P\to\{0,\dots,\lgt(N)\}.
\]
Then $|V|\le(\lgt(N)+1)^{|P|}\le(\lgt(N)+1)^{\nfl{y}}$.

Now let $0<n\le N$ be $y$-smooth.
\begin{itemize}
\item For $p\in P$ we have $2^{v_p(n)}\le p^{v_p(n)}\le n\le N$, hence $v_p(n)\le\lgt(N)$.
\item Every prime factor $q$ of $n$ satisfies $q\le y$. Since $q$ is an integer, $q\le\nfl{y}$, so $q\in P$.
\item Primes $p\in P$ that do not divide $n$ have $v_p(n)=0$.
\end{itemize}
Hence $n=\prod_{p\in\pf(n)}p^{v_p(n)}=\prod_{p\in P}p^{v_p(n)}\in V$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{prod_smooth_bounded}]\label{lem:prod_smooth_bounded}
Let $Q\subset\N$ be finite, $y\in\R$, $B\in\N$. Suppose each $p\in Q$ satisfies
\begin{itemize}
\item $0<p-1$ (truncated subtraction),
\item $p-1\le B$,
\item $p-1$ is $y$-smooth.
\end{itemize}
Then $0<\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)\le B^{|Q|}$, and $\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$ is $y$-smooth.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Positivity and the bound $\prod(p-1)\le\prod B=B^{|Q|}$ are immediate.

For smoothness, let $q$ be a prime factor of the product. Since $q$ is prime, it divides some factor $p-1$ with $p\in Q$. As $p-1\neq0$, $q$ is a prime factor of $p-1$, so $q\le y$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{choose_ge_pow_div_aiprover_s9}]\label{lem:choose_ge_pow_div_aiprover_s9}
For $M,k\in\N$ with $k\le M$, $(M/k)^k\le\binom Mk$ in $\R$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $k=0$ both sides equal $1$, using the convention $M/0=0$ and $0^0=1$.

Let $k\ge1$. Then $M/k\ge1$, so for $0\le j<k$,
\[
 M-j\ \ge\ M-\frac Mk\,j=\frac Mk\,(k-j).
\]
Both sides are nonnegative. Multiplying over $j<k$ gives
\[
 k!\binom Mk=\prod_{j<k}(M-j)\ \ge\ \Bigl(\frac Mk\Bigr)^k\prod_{j<k}(k-j)=\Bigl(\frac Mk\Bigr)^k k!.
\]
Dividing by $k!$ gives the claim. This is the source's argument, with each factor of $\prod_j (M-j)/(k-j)$ at least $M/k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{choose_ge_pow_div}]\label{lem:choose_ge_pow_div}
For $M,k\in\N$ with $k\le M$, $(M/k)^k\le\binom Mk$ in $\R$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{choose\_ge\_pow\_div} --- proved by AIProver, job 1, session 9}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_log_ineq_growth_logbound}]\label{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth_logbound}
For real $\delta>0$ and $x\ge1$,
\[
 \log\Bigl(\frac{x^{2\delta}\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)\le\log11+(2\delta+1)\log x.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first establish two numerical facts.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\log5<2$. Indeed $e^2\ge\sum_{m<8}2^m/m!=155/21>5$.
\item $\log2>3/10$. Indeed $e^{3/10}$ is at most $\sum_{m<8}(3/10)^m/m!$ plus the Taylor remainder bound $(3/10)^8\cdot 9/(8!\cdot8)$, and this total is $<2$.
\end{itemize}

From $\log x\le x-1$ and $10\log2-1\ge2$ we get
\[
 \log(5x)=\log5+\log x\le2+x\le10\log2\cdot x.
\]
Therefore $\log(5x)/\log2\le10x$. Since $x^{2\delta}\ge1$ we also have $x^{-2\delta}\le1$.

Now write the argument of the logarithm as
\[
 x^{2\delta}\Bigl(\frac{\log(5x)}{\log2}+x^{-2\delta}\Bigr).
\]
The second factor lies in $(0,11x]$. Taking logarithms,
\[
 \log(\cdots)\le2\delta\log x+\log(11x)=\log11+(2\delta+1)\log x.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_log_ineq_growth_eventually}]\label{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth_eventually}
Let $\delta>0$ and $a,b\in\R$. There is $x_0\ge1$ such that every $x\ge x_0$ satisfies $a\le\log x$ and $b\le x^\delta$.
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
Both $\log x$ and $x^\delta$ tend to $+\infty$. Take $x_0$ to be the maximum of $1$ and the two thresholds.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_log_ineq_growth}]\label{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth}
Let $\delta>0$, $c\in\R$, $d>0$. There is $x_0\ge1$ such that for all $x\ge x_0$,
\[
 x^\delta\log\Bigl(\frac{x^{2\delta}\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)+c\,x^{2\delta}<d\,x^{2\delta}\log x.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth_eventually} with
\[
 a=\max\bigl(1,\,2(|c|+1)/d\bigr),\qquad b=2(\log11+2\delta+1)/d.
\]
For $x\ge x_0$ put $u=x^\delta>0$ and $L=\log x$. Then
\[
 L\ge1,\qquad dL\ge2(|c|+1),\qquad du\ge2(\log11+2\delta+1).
\]
Splitting $duL$ into two halves gives
\[
 duL\ \ge\ (\log11+2\delta+1)L+(|c|+1)u\ >\ \log11+(2\delta+1)L+cu.
\]
The last step uses $\log 11\cdot L\ge\log11$ and $(|c|+1)u>cu$.

Multiply by $u$ and use $x^{2\delta}=u^2$. \Cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth_logbound} bounds $u$ times the logarithm by $u(\log11+(2\delta+1)L)$. Combining the two inequalities gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_log_ineq_pointwise}]\label{lem:asymp_log_ineq_pointwise}
Let $\delta,\varepsilon,x\in\R$ with $\delta>0$, $4\delta<\varepsilon<1$, and $x>1$. Assume
\[
 x^\delta\log\Bigl(\frac{x^{2\delta}\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)+(1-\varepsilon)\log5\cdot x^{2\delta}<\frac\delta2\,x^{2\delta}\log x.
\]
Let $k=\nfl{x^{2\delta}}$. Then $1\le k\le x^{1-\delta}$ and
\[
 x^\delta\log\Bigl(\frac{k\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)+(1-\varepsilon)k\log(5x)<k\log\bigl(x^{1-\delta}/k\bigr).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Bounds on $k$.} Since $x^{2\delta}\ge1$ we get $k\ge1$. Also $k\le x^{2\delta}<k+1$. Since $3\delta<1$, $x^{2\delta}\le x^{1-\delta}$.

\emph{The two sides.} Put $L=\log x>0$. Monotonicity of $\log$ and $k\le x^{2\delta}$ bound the first term on the left by the corresponding term in the hypothesis. Moreover $\log k\le2\delta L$, so
\[
 k\log(x^{1-\delta}/k)=k\bigl((1-\delta)L-\log k\bigr)\ge(1-3\delta)kL.
\]
Using $\log(5x)=\log5+L$, the hypothesis, and $(1-\varepsilon)\log5\cdot k\le(1-\varepsilon)\log5\cdot x^{2\delta}$, the left side is
\[
 <\ \frac\delta2x^{2\delta}L+(1-\varepsilon)kL\ \le\ \frac\delta2(k+1)L+(1-\varepsilon)kL.
\]

\emph{Comparison.} It remains to check $\frac\delta2(k+1)+(1-\varepsilon)k\le(1-3\delta)k$. This holds because
\[
 (\varepsilon-3\delta)k-\frac\delta2(k+1)=(\varepsilon-4\delta)k+\frac\delta2(k-1)\ge0.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_log_ineq}]\label{lem:asymp_log_ineq}
Let $\delta>0$ and $4\delta<\varepsilon<1$. There is $x_0\ge1$ such that for all $x\ge x_0$, with $k=\nfl{x^{2\delta}}$, we have $1\le k\le x^{1-\delta}$ and
\[
 x^\delta\log\Bigl(\frac{k\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)+(1-\varepsilon)k\log(5x)<k\log(x^{1-\delta}/k).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Take $x_0'$ from \cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq_growth} with $c=(1-\varepsilon)\log5$ and $d=\delta/2$, and set $x_0=x_0'+1$. Then every $x\ge x_0$ satisfies $x>1$ and the hypothesis of \cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq_pointwise}, which gives the conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_main_lhs_log}]\label{lem:asymp_main_lhs_log}
Let $x,\delta,\varepsilon\in\R$ and $k\in\N$ with $x\ge1$ and $\varepsilon<1$. Put $N=\nfl{5x}$ and
\[
 A=(\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}\cdot(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}.
\]
Then $A>0$ and
\[
 \log A\le x^\delta\log\Bigl(\frac{k\log(5x)}{\log2}+1\Bigr)+(1-\varepsilon)k\log(5x).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $5x\ge5$ we have $N\ge1$, so $A>0$, and
\[
 \log A=\nfl{x^\delta}\log(\lgt(N^k)+1)+(1-\varepsilon)k\log N.
\]
\begin{itemize}
\item From $2^{\lgt(N^k)}\le N^k$ we get $\lgt(N^k)\le k\log N/\log2\le k\log(5x)/\log2$, using $N\le5x$.
\item $0\le\nfl{x^\delta}\le x^\delta$, and the logarithm $\log\bigl(k\log(5x)/\log2+1\bigr)$ is nonnegative.
\item $\log N\le\log(5x)$ and $1-\varepsilon\ge0$.
\end{itemize}
Substituting these bounds term by term gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_main_rhs_log}]\label{lem:asymp_main_rhs_log}
Let $x,\delta\in\R$ and $k,M\in\N$ with $1\le k$, $k\le x^{1-\delta}$ and $x^{1-\delta}\le M$. Then
\[
 k\log(x^{1-\delta}/k)\le\log\binom Mk.
\]
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{n+nb+bp}{\PYZus{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb+bp}{≤}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{Real}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{log}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{(}\PY{n}{M}\PY{n+nb+bp}{.}\PY{n}{choose}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{k}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{ℝ}\PY{o}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{:=}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{h\PYZus{}log\PYZus{}choose\PYZus{}ge}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
We have $k\le M$, $0<x^{1-\delta}/k\le M/k$, and $\binom Mk>0$. Hence, using \cref{lem:choose_ge_pow_div} in the last step,
\[
 k\log(x^{1-\delta}/k)\le k\log(M/k)=\log\bigl((M/k)^k\bigr)\le\log\binom Mk.\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{asymp_main}]\label{lem:asymp_main}
Let $\delta>0$ and $4\delta<\varepsilon<1$. There is $x_0\ge1$ with the following property. Let $x\ge x_0$, put $k=\nfl{x^{2\delta}}$ and $N=\nfl{5x}$, and let $M\in\N$ with $x^{1-\delta}\le M$. Then $1\le k\le M$ and
\[
 (\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}\,(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}<\binom Mk.
\]
\end{lemma}

\PY{+w}{  }\PY{n}{linarith}

\begin{proof}
Take $x_0$ from \cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq}. That lemma gives $1\le k\le x^{1-\delta}\le M$. Both sides of the desired inequality are positive, the left by \cref{lem:asymp_main_lhs_log} and the right because $k\le M$. So it suffices to compare logarithms. Chain three inequalities:
\begin{itemize}
\item the upper bound of \cref{lem:asymp_main_lhs_log} for the logarithm of the left side;
\item the strict inequality of \cref{lem:asymp_log_ineq};
\item the lower bound of \cref{lem:asymp_main_rhs_log} for $\log\binom Mk$.
\end{itemize}
This is the formal version of comparing (1) and (2).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{exists_prime_finset}]\label{lem:exists_prime_finset}
Assume (H). Let $\delta,\eta\in\R$ with $0<\delta<1/4$ and $\eta>0$. There is $x_0$ such that for every $x\ge x_0$ there is a finite set $S\subset\N$ with $|S|\ge x^{1-\eta}$ in which every $p$ satisfies:
\begin{itemize}
\item $p$ is prime;
\item $2x<p\le5x$;
\item $p-1$ is $x^\delta$-smooth.
\end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\end{leanblock}
\begin{proof}
Apply (H) with $\delta$ and $\eta/2$ to obtain $x_0'$, and set $x_0=\max(x_0',1)$. For $x\ge x_0$, the set $A$ of primes counted in (H) is contained in $\{0,\dots,\nfl{5x}\}$, so it is finite. Take $S=A$. Then
\[
 |S|\ge x^{1-\eta/2}\ge x^{1-\eta},
\]
because $x\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{good_n_from_finset}]\label{lem:good_n_from_finset}
Let $S\subset\N$ be finite, $x,\delta,\varepsilon\in\R$, and $k\in\N$, with $x\ge1$ and $\varepsilon<1$. Suppose every $p\in S$ is prime, satisfies $2x<p\le5x$, and has $p-1$ being $x^\delta$-smooth. Suppose also $1\le k\le|S|$. Put $N=\nfl{5x}$ and assume
\[
 (\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}\,(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}<\binom{|S|}{k}.
\]
Then there is $n\in\N$ with $x<n$, $0<n$ and $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{leanblock}{Lean: \texttt{good\_n\_from\_finset} --- proved by the captain after a hand-back}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Setting up the pigeonhole.} Each $p\in S$ satisfies $p\ge2$ and $p\le N$, since $p$ is an integer $\le5x$. Thus $0<p-1\le N$, and $p-1$ is $x^\delta$-smooth.

Let $V$ be the set given by \cref{lem:smooth_count} for $N^k$ and $y=x^\delta$. By \cref{lem:prod_smooth_bounded}, every $k$-subset $Q\subseteq S$ has $\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$ positive, at most $N^k$, and $x^\delta$-smooth, so this product lies in $V$.

\emph{Applying the pigeonhole.} \Cref{lem:exists_large_fiber} gives $Q\subseteq S$ with $|Q|=k$ and $\binom{|S|}k\le|V|\,g(n)$, where $n=\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$. By \cref{lem:prod_smooth_bounded}, $0<n\le N^k$.

\emph{The bound $n>x$.} Since $k\ge1$, $Q$ contains some $p_0$. Then $p_0-1\mid n$, so
\[
 n\ge p_0-1>2x-1\ge x.
\]

\emph{The bound on $g(n)$.} Suppose $g(n)\le n^{1-\varepsilon}$. Since $1-\varepsilon\ge0$ we have $n^{1-\varepsilon}\le(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}$. Also $|V|\le(\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}$. Then
\[
 |V|\,g(n)\le|V|\,(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}\le(\lgt(N^k)+1)^{\nfl{x^\delta}}(N^k)^{1-\varepsilon}<\binom{|S|}k\le|V|\,g(n),
\]
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{exists_good_n_large}]\label{lem:exists_good_n_large}
Assume (H). Let $0<\varepsilon<1$ and $C\in\R$. There is $n\in\N$ with $C<n$, $0<n$ and $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Set $\delta=\varepsilon/8$. Then $0<\delta<1/4$ and $4\delta<\varepsilon$.

Let $x_0$ come from \cref{lem:exists_prime_finset} with $\eta=\delta$, and $x_1$ from \cref{lem:asymp_main}. Choose
\[
 x\ge\max(x_0,x_1,C,1).
\]
\Cref{lem:exists_prime_finset} provides $S$ with $|S|\ge x^{1-\delta}$. \Cref{lem:asymp_main} with $M=|S|$ and $k=\nfl{x^{2\delta}}$ gives $1\le k\le|S|$ and the inequality required by \cref{lem:good_n_from_finset}. That lemma yields $n$ with $n>x\ge C$, $n>0$ and $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[\leanname{infinite_of_lt_one}]\label{lem:infinite_of_lt_one}
Assume (H) and $0<\varepsilon<1$. Then $\{n\in\N:\ 0<n,\ g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}\}$ is infinite.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If the set were finite, it would be bounded above by some $M\in\N$. \Cref{lem:exists_good_n_large} with $C=\max(M,0)$ produces an element $n>M$ of the set, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}
Assume (H) and let $\varepsilon>0$.

If $\varepsilon<1$, the claim is \cref{lem:infinite_of_lt_one}.

If $\varepsilon\ge1$, apply \cref{lem:infinite_of_lt_one} with $\varepsilon'=1/2$. For $n\ge1$ the map $t\mapsto n^t$ is nondecreasing, and $1-\varepsilon\le0<1/2$. Hence every $n>0$ with $g(n)>n^{1/2}$ also satisfies
\[
 g(n)>n^{1/2}\ge n^{1-\varepsilon}.
\]
The infinite set for $\varepsilon'=1/2$ is therefore contained in the set for $\varepsilon$, which is consequently infinite.
\end{proof}

\section{Independent review}

A referee model (claude-opus-5-5), which took no part in the formalization or the proof, compared the verified Lean file with the source. Verdict: \textbf{FAITHFUL}.

\paragraph{Definitions.} \leanname{totientFiberCard} encodes $g(n)=\#\{m\ge1:\varphi(m)=n\}$ as the \leanname{Nat.card} of the subtype $\{m : 0<m \wedge \varphi(m)=n\}$. \leanname{Nat.card} returns $0$ on an infinite type. That case does not arise, because the file proves the fiber finite (\leanname{totient_fiber_finite}). At $n=0$ the fiber is empty, since $\varphi(m)=0$ only for $m=0$. This agrees with the source, which only considers positive $n$.

\leanname{IsSmoothNat} $m$ $y$ says that every prime factor of $m$ is at most $y$. For $m>1$ this is exactly $P^+(m)\le y$. For $m=1$ it holds vacuously, which matches the convention $P^+(1)=1$. In the hypothesis this matters only for $p=2$, which affects nothing.

\leanname{SmoothShiftedPrimesHypothesis} is Hypothesis (H) verbatim, with real $x$ and real powers. It says: for all real $\delta\in(0,1/4)$ and $\eta>0$ there is $x_0$ such that for all $x\ge x_0$,
\[
\#\{p \text{ prime} : 2x<p\le 5x,\ p-1 \text{ is } x^\delta\text{-smooth}\}\ \ge\ x^{1-\eta}.
\]
The counted set is bounded by $5x$, so \leanname{Nat.card} is the true cardinality. The hypothesis is not trivially false: it is a genuine smooth-shifted-primes statement. It is therefore not a vacuous assumption.

\paragraph{Statement.} The theorem assumes \leanname{SmoothShiftedPrimesHypothesis} and any real $\varepsilon>0$. It concludes that $\{n\in\mathbb N : 0<n \wedge g(n) > n^{1-\varepsilon}\}$ is infinite. This is exactly the source theorem: it is conditional on (H), the quantification is over all $\varepsilon>0$, and the inequality is strict.

There are no extra hypotheses. Nothing is weakened. Nothing needs to be supplied to recover the source statement.

The parenthetical Lemma 1 is not part of the target statement. Only its finiteness half appears, as the auxiliary lemma \leanname{totient_fiber_finite}. The bound $g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}$ is not formalized. It is not needed for the main conclusion.

\paragraph{Proof.} The formal proof follows the source's product-and-pigeonhole argument, with different scaling and constants.

\leanname{exists_prime_finset} applies (H) with $\eta/2$. It extracts a finset $S$ of primes $p\in(2x,5x]$ with $p-1$ being $x^\delta$-smooth and $|S|\ge x^{1-\eta}$. The proof takes $\delta=\eta=\varepsilon/8$ and $k=\lfloor x^{2\delta}\rfloor$.

\leanname{choose_ge_pow_div} proves $\binom Mk\ge (M/k)^k$. This is inequality (1).

\leanname{smooth_count} bounds the number of $y$-smooth integers in $[1,N]$ by $(\lfloor\log_2 N\rfloor+1)^{\lfloor y\rfloor}$. This is inequality (2), in a slightly sharper form.

\leanname{totient_prod_primes} gives $\varphi\bigl(\prod_{p\in Q} p\bigr)=\prod_{p\in Q}(p-1)$. \leanname{prod_primes_inj} is unique factorization. \leanname{prod_smooth_bounded} shows that the product of the shifted primes is smooth and at most $N^k$, where $N=\lfloor 5x\rfloor$.

\leanname{exists_large_fiber} is the pigeonhole step:
\[
\binom{|S|}{k}\le |V|\cdot g(n)
\]
for some value $n$.

The asymptotic lemmas (\leanname{asymp_log_ineq_growth}, \leanname{asymp_log_ineq_pointwise}, \leanname{asymp_main}) make the source's $o(k)$ estimate explicit by comparing logarithms. They show
\[
|V|\cdot (N^k)^{1-\varepsilon} < \binom{M}{k}
\]
for large $x$, using $4\delta<\varepsilon$. \leanname{good_n_from_finset} combines these to obtain $n$ with $g(n)>n^{1-\varepsilon}$.

One deviation from the source: infinitude is derived from $n\ge p_0-1>x$, where $p_0$ is a prime in the chosen subset. The source instead appeals to the upper bound in Lemma 1. The formal route is simpler and valid.

\leanname{infinite_of_lt_one} turns arbitrarily large solutions into an infinite set. The theorem \leanname{solution} handles $\varepsilon\ge1$ by monotonicity from $\varepsilon=1/2$, using $n^{1-\varepsilon}\le n^{1/2}$ for $n\ge1$, as in the source.

All auxiliary lemmas are stated at least as strongly as the steps they represent.

\paragraph{Concerns.} \begin{itemize}
\item The quantitative bound $g(n)\ll_\eta n^{1+\eta}$ from Lemma 1 is not formalized. Only the finiteness of fibers is proved. The main theorem does not depend on the bound.
\item The result is conditional on (H), exactly as in the source. The formalization does not establish (H).
\end{itemize}
